Retrieved from [http://cimss.ssec.wisc.edu/goes/OCLOFactSheetPDFs/ABIQuickGuide_CIMSSRGB_v2.pdf](http://cimss.ssec.wisc.edu/goes/OCLOFactSheetPDFs/ABIQuickGuide_CIMSSRGB_v2.pdf)
8.  Vermote, E., & NOAA CDR program. (2018). NOAA climate data record (CDR) of AVHRR Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI), Version 5 [Data set]. NOAA National Centers for Environmental Information. [https://doi.org/10.7289/V5ZG6QH9](https://doi.org/10.7289/V5ZG6QH9)

# 10
EXPORTING WITH EASE

> Of increasing importance, researchers are now required to archive their data after it has undergone analysis and visualization. Some journals and grants now require scientists to make their data publicly available to ensure that the conclusions are reproducible and transparent. This chapter will show some common ways to store data, whether it be as publication‐quality images, text files, or netCDF4\. For self‐describing data, this chapter provides an overview of some important conventions to make data easier to distribute, share, and archive.

It is very likely that your Python projects will generate data or imagery that you will want to save for future use, either for yourself or to share with others. Furthermore, many public grants and research journals require authors to save data for five years. There are numerous formats you can use to save your data and visualizations using Python. Plots can be saved in jpeg, png, or pdf. Binary files ([Section 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c3-sec-0007)) may not be human readable, but they have good compression and are easy to import and manipulate using packages like **NumPy.** Text files (like csv or tsv, [Section 3.2.2](#c03.xhtml_c3-sec-0008)) are adequate when the dataset is small and needs to be readable by others. However, neither are good for long‐term storage nor sharing. As discussed in [Section 3.2.3](#c03.xhtml_c3-sec-0009), **netCDF** and **HDF** are useful for long‐term archiving of results, are cross platform, and are readable outside of the Python environment. These formats support metadata storage and can be read on MacOs, Windows, and Linux operating systems. Self‐describing formats are easier to disseminate within your research community, and if you add descripting metadata, you can more easily return to your results after extended periods of time.

In this chapter, I will show how you can export data to text, pickle, and netCDF using common packages. The examples in this chapter will use **NumPy, Pandas, netCDF4,** and **xarray,** which you have learned about extensively in other chapters. Not only do these packages allow you to import data, but they can also save data as well. I will also introduce two new packages, **csv** and **pickle**, which can archive text and binary formatted, data, respectively. Figures can be saved using `matplotlib.pyplot.`

## 10.1 Figures

Saving plots is relatively straightforward. In [Chapter 6](#c06.xhtml), you learned to end matplotlib code segments with `plt.show().` To write the image to file, I use the `plt.savefig()` command, followed by `plt.close().`

```
 plt.figure(figsize=[5,5]) 
plt.hist(fires['frp(MW)'], bins=bins10MW)

plt.savefig('histogram.png', bbox_inches='tight') 
plt.close()
```

The `plt.close()` command tells Jupyter Notebook to run in **noninteractive mode**, so no figure will render. Instead, a png image named `histogram.png` is saved in your default directory, which is where you launched Jupyter Notebook by default. If you want to save the image somewhere else, you can include either an absolute or local relative file path before the filename. `plt.savefig` will automatically detect the file format from the extension. The second argument ( `bbox_inches` ) refers to the whitespace around the plot, so it is aesthetic and optional. If you leave it out, the canvas will have a lot of whitespace surrounding the plot. Adding `bbox_inches=’tight’` option will fit the canvas to the plot more closely. You can alternatively specify the whitespace you want. For example, `bbox_inches=[1,1]` will add 1 inch of whitespace around the plot. Note that the tight layout only includes tick labels, axis labels, and titles. If you have other elements outside of the plot, they may overlap or be cropped from the image.

Image file formats like PNG, JPG, and GIF are **rasterized** – that is, they hold information in pixel space ([Table 10.1](#c10.xhtml_c10-tbl-0001)). JPG and PNG formats are better for images with many colors. PNG is a good choice because it can support unlimited colors, with a moderate file size. If there are only a few colors (such as for line or scatter plots), GIF are a good choice because the file size is the smallest.

If you wish to create simple animations, GIF can easily be converted into animated GIFs either through online or software tools. For publication quality figures, **vector** images are preferred because they can be zoomed in indefinitely without a loss of quality and usually have smaller file sizes. The most common formats are SVG and PDF. Today, many journals require authors to submit their figures as vector graphics. I recommend PDF because they are platform independent and there are many tools to read them, including most web browsers. PDFs can be larger than PNGs for complex satellite imagery. If storage space is not a limitation for you, it might be helpful to save the figure in both a raster and vectorized format.

| Characteristic | JPG | GIF | PNG |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| colors | millions | 256 | unlimited |
| compression | lossy | lossless | lossless |
| transparency | no | yes | yes |
| animation | no | yes | no |
| file size | large | small | medium |

[**Table 10.1**](#c10.xhtml_R_c10-tbl-0001) Differences between Image Types

## 10.2 Text Files

I briefly looked at the `open` command back in Section 5.1.1\. I described it as a useful method of opening an irregular text file for reading. I can also use the same command to write files as well. `Open` can write both text and binary files, but below I will only show a text example since there are simpler methods for saving binary files (which I’ll show in the next section). Suppose I have three lists of items to write to file:

```
 name = ['GOES-16', 'IceSat-2', 'Himawari']
agency = ['NOAA', 'NASA', 'JAXA']
orbit = ['GEO', 'LEO', 'GEO']
```

The following code is a loop and must be run all at once. I first import the csv library to facilitate writing the file. The `writer` command defines the file and how to separate the data. Then `writerow` assigns the data. You can pass either a single string of text (as I did with the headers), or create a loop to write items from a list to the file.

```
 import csv

with open('satellite.txt', 'w', newline='') as csvfile:
 writer = csv.writer(csvfile, delimiter=',')

 # adds a header
 writer.writerow(['name', 'agency', 'orbit'])

 # Writes each element as a single row
 for i in range(0, len(name)):
 writer.writerow([name[i], agency[i], orbit[i]])
```

The above command is useful if you want to have significant control over the layout and formatting of the final file. If you want to quickly save a readable file, using the `to_csv` command in Pandas is convenient. The option `index` = `False` suppresses the indices of the DataFrame (which are printed to the left of the DataFrame) from being printed to file.

```
 import pandas as pd
df = pd.DataFrame({'name': name,'agency': agency,
'orbit': orbit})

df.to_csv('satellites.csv', index=False)
```

## 10.3 Pickling

In Python, **pickling** is a method of storing serialized binary data for later use. Pickle files ( `.pkl` ) can store any type of object. Pickling gets its name from the culinary practice of salting food to preserve and store for long periods of time.

Below, I import the **pickle** library and use the `pkl.dump` command by passing the DataFrame name (note that any object can be pickled) and create a new file called `satellites.p` using `open` . Instead of `‘w’` , I use `‘wb’` to indicate write binary.

```
 import pickle as pkl
pkl.dump(satellites, open("satellites.p", "wb"))
```

The file should be saved in your default location. If I want to open the saved file, I use the open command with `‘rb’` (read binary) and `pkl.load` :

```
 satellites2 = pkl.load(open("satellites.p", "rb"))
```

## 10.4 NumPy Binary Files

Like pickle files, NumPy binary files ( `.npz` ) are also binary files but more geared toward arrays, which can be multidimensional. You can also conveniently can store multiple datasets inside a single file. For instance, I will save both the name and orbit in a file called `satnames.npz` (the extension is automatically added). The `name=` , `agency=` , and `orbit=` are arbitrary names and optional. However, I recommend including them because otherwise NumPy will assign generic labels to the data:

```
 import numpy as np
np.savez('satnames', name=name, agency=agency, orbit=orbit)
```

Now, I can reopen the file using the `np.load` command and assign it to a variable called `npzfiles` . I can print the available arrays inside `npzfile` using the `.files` attribute. Since I stored two arrays inside the file, this returns two values, name and orbit. Then use the `npzfile[‘name’]` command to access the list:

```
 npzfile = np.load('satnames.npz')
npzfile.files
['name', ‘agency’, 'orbit']

npzfile['name']
array(['GOES-16', 'IceSat-2', 'Himawari'], 
dtype='<U8')
```

For a typical scientific project, I likely already have NumPy imported. Thus, using the npz format over pkl means I have one fewer dependency. In terms of file size, they are not significantly different. However, they are best used as **intermediate files**, which are data saved prior to the completion of the main program. Intermediate files can help speed up processing if there are calculations that do not need to be dynamically calculated. However, I do not recommend either format for data that you wish to share because pkl and npz are not self‐describing formats and are not easily read without Python.

## 10.5 NetCDF

Because they are so prevalent in Earth science, much of this book discusses how to read and understand the structure of netCDF files. Similar to reading netCDF files, the process of creating a new netCDF file can be tedious, but it is worth investing the time to learn how the files can be organized. To accomplish this, I will first use the more verbose netCDF4 package. However, afterward, I will demonstrate how you can quickly (and with fewer lines of code) create netCDF files using xarray.

### 10.5.1 Using netCDF4 to Create netCDF Files

In the following example, I will import multiple JPSS AOD files and combine them. Swath data are often stored into one‐minute scans, and it can be helpful to combine them. Additionally, I will quality control the data using the quality flag variable.

So, first I import the requisite libraries. We have not used glob package in this text, glob will return a list of files that contain a given string or character pattern. You will notice that the files we are searching for end in asterisk (*). This tells to the command to search for netCDF files that begin with JRR-AOD, regardless of what character pattern that follows.

```
 from netCDF4 import Dataset
import numpy as np
from glob import glob
```

Next, I search for all files in the directory:

```
 # Get file names, exit if no files are found
filenames = glob('data/aod/JRR-AOD*.nc')

# Import datasets
file_id = Dataset(filenames[0], mode='r')
```

I want to create a new netCDF file, so I need to know the dimensions of the final dataset. There are many variables in the file, but since I want to save just the best quality AOD550, the only relevant variables are `Latitude` , `Longitude` , `AOD550` , and `QCAll` . I can ignore the rest to help reduce the file size. Below, I am printing dimensions names, and I will show that they are all the same:

```
 for var in ["Latitude", "Longitude", "AOD550", "QCAll"]:
  print(var, file_id.variables[var].dimensions)
Latitude ('Rows', 'Columns')
Longitude ('Rows', 'Columns')
AOD550 ('Rows', 'Columns')
QCAll ('Rows', 'Columns')
```

Since I am combining three files, I will have a final dataset that is bigger than the original in one dimension. Below I make a list of the dimension sizes in the first file. In this example, all three files have the same dimension sizes. This is a characteristic of many VIIRS and ABI products, but will not be true of all datasets.

```
 for dim in ['Columns', 'Rows']:
  print(dim, len(file_id.dimensions[dim]))
Columns 3200
Rows 768
```

Next, let’s import the data above into a variable and count the number of files:

```
 cols = len(file_id.dimensions['Columns'])
rows = len(file_id.dimensions['Rows'])

num_files = len(filenames)
file_id.close()

print(rows, cols, num_files)
768 3200 3
```

Now that I have information on the size, I can create the netCDF file. I will use the `Dataset` module from the netcdf4 package but will use the `“w”` argument to write rather than read (which, if you recall, uses the “r” argument). I write a general description of what is in the data using `.description` , which assigns the text string to the global metadata.

```
 # Create output file name
output_filename = "JRR-AOD-combined.nc"

# Open a new netCDF file 
rootgrp = Dataset(output_filename, "w", format="NETCDF4")
rootgrp.description = "Combined NOAA-20 AOD swaths for Alberta Fire"
```

Before I can fill the dataset, I need to create the dimensions ( `.createDimensions` ) and variables ( `.createVariables` ). Because swath data are scanned along the north–south direction, I will concatenate the data in the row dimension. The size of the column dimension will remain unchanged but the latitude dimension will be three times the original size. Note that if the files had irregular dimensions, I would have to read in all the files to get the individual dimension sizes and potentially grid them to a new multidimensional array using a gridding technique from [Chapter 8](#c08.xhtml).

```
 # Format the latitude and longitude variables
lat = rootgrp.createDimension("lat", rows*num_files)
lon = rootgrp.createDimension("lon", cols)
```

The two lines above define the dimensions, `lat` and `lon` . Note that some other datasets may have time and height as well. When creating the latitude and longitude dimensions, you must provide single integer value length, such are `rows*num_files` and `cols` , respectively. Next, I create the variables:

```
 latitudes = rootgrp.createVariable("Latitude","f4", ("lat","lon"), \
zlib = True, least_significant_digit = 2)
longitudes = rootgrp.createVariable("Longitude","f4", ("lat","lon"), \
zlib = True, least_significant_digit = 2)

variable = rootgrp.createVariable('AOD550', "f4", ("lat","lon"), \
zlib = True, least_significant_digit = 2, fill_value = -999.9)
```

These three lines are the placeholder for the `latitude` , `longitude` , and `variable` (which is AOD). When I create the variable, I will create a two‐dimensional array since the coordinates are irregularly spaced. If the data were evenly spaced, then a single‐valued array would suffice for latitude and longitude, although AOD would still be two‐dimensional. I assign the variable a name, as it will appear inside the netCDF file, along with a number type, and the two coordinate dimensions. The last two keywords are optional but are for compressing the dataset.

If you are a Linux user, you may be familiar with gzip and tar, which are the primary compression and archiving utilities. Gzip and tar are both wrappers for `zlib` , a free data compression library that uses an algorithm to “deflate” the data to reduce the file size. For text, `zlib` can reduce file sizes up to 50%, although binary‐based datasets have lower compression rates. Without `zlib=True` in the code above, the final netCDF file size will be much larger.

The option `least_significant_digit` is for `zlib,` which indicates the smallest decimal place of the variable that is precise. In the example, I use a value of 2 so that the data are saved to two decimal places. Smaller values indicate more compression (reducing file size) but will increase the time required to save the file. The opposite is true for larger values.

In the variable group for `AOD550` , I also assign a `fill_value` ([Section 3.2.3](#c03.xhtml_c3-sec-0009)) of ‐999.9 for indicating where the data should be ignored, because there was no retrieval made for that point.

Once the placeholders for the variables are defined, I can also assign metadata. The standard units for latitude and longitude are degrees north and east, respectively. AOD is unitless, so none are assigned in it’s metadata.

```
 latitudes.units = "degrees north"
longitudes.units = "degrees east"
```

The following code combines the three files. Each file is opened and the latitude, longitude, and AOD550 are read in from the file. Because I am combining three files, I define three zero arrays that are three times the size of the first file. I will open each file and iteratively fill with values. In addition to combining the files, I will only save the “best” quality data. So, I will examine the `QCAll` variable and only include data where this column is equal to 0\. Other numbers indicate medium, low quality, or no retrievals. All other data will be set to the fill value.

```
 var = np.zeros((rows*num_files, cols))
lats = np.zeros((rows*num_files, cols))
lons = np.zeros((rows*num_files, cols))

for filenum, filename in enumerate(filenames):
 file_id = Dataset(filename, mode='r')

 i1 = rows*filenum
 i2 = rows*(filenum+1)

 # Get coordinates
 lats[i1:i2, 0:cols] = file_id.variables['Latitude'][:,:]
 lons[i1:i2, 0:cols] = file_id.variables['Longitude'][:,:]

 # Create and fill variables
 value = file_id.variables['AOD550'][:,:]

 # Simple binary quality flag
 dqf = file_id.variables[ 'QCAll'][:,:]
 value[ dqf != 0 ] = -999.9

 var[i1:i2, :] = value[:,:]

 file_id.close()
```

I fill the new netCDF file with the combined data.

```
 # Populate lat/lon/var
latitudes[:,:] = lats
longitudes[:,:] = lons
variable[:,:] = var
```

And finally, I close the file. Without this last step, the file will not be readable by any external programs.

```
 # Close original/modified netCDF file
rootgrp.close()
```

### 10.5.2 Using Xarray to Create netCDF Files

Following the previous section, you may appreciate the simplicity that xarray offers for saving netCDF files. First, I can open and combine all files using a single line of code. In the netCDF4 method, I needed to create a loop to read the data. As in the previous example, I am stacking the rows to combine the three datasets, which in xarray is done using the `concat_dim=’Rows’` and `combine= ’nested’` keywords. On the third line, I use the `where` command to quality control the data and keep only the “best‐quality” data.

```
 import xarray as xr

fname = 'data/aod/JRR-AOD*.nc'
aod = xr.open_mfdataset(fname, concat_dim='Rows', combine='nested')
filtered = aod['AOD550'].where(aod['QCAll'] == 0)
```

With the single line `.to_necdf` , I can save the file.

```
 filtered.to_netcdf('JRR-AOD-xarray-combined.nc')
```

The netCDF4 method of saving data are highly verbose, while the xarray code is more compact. Additionally, all the metadata from the source netcdf file are conveniently passed to the final file, so the code is very succinct compared to numerous steps that are necessary using the netCDF4 package method. If you wish to delete some of the metadata, note that you can do so using the del command. For example, you can use the following command to delete the units attribute:

```
 del(filtered.attrs['units'])
```

While xarray is more compact, the xarray package uses the netCDF4 package as a dependency. So, a working knowledge of the netcdf4 package is useful for understanding and customizing more advanced features in xarray.

### 10.5.3 Following Climate and Forecast (CF) Metadata Conventions

For long‐term archives, I encourage you to follow netCDF conventions. This facilitates data transparency and will make the data easier to interpret across platforms and over time. While following Climate and Forecast (CF) metadata conventions can be quite in depth, here are some important rules:

**Coordinates:**

*   Make coordinate variables for every dimension when possible.
*   Use an existing netCDF Convention for your coordinate variables, especially to identify spatio‐temporal coordinates.
*   Use the same dimensions when two variables use the same coordinates. However, make a new dimension if two variables have the same shape (perhaps by coincidence, both have 12 vertical dimensions) but use a different coordinate scheme.

**Variable grouping:**

*   All values inside a variable should have the same type (numeric, integer, byte, etc.) and units.
*   Attributes should apply to the entire variable.
*   Dimensions should be spatial and temporal coordinates, if possible.

**Attributes:**

*   Where possible, add variable attributes for `units` , `long_name` (e.g., a name you would label a plot with), and `_fillValue` . You can also set the `valid_ range` in addition or instead of `_fillValue` .
*   If an attribute applies to the whole dataset, set it to be a global attribute.
*   Encode time as an interval from a reference time, such as `‘seconds since 1970‐01‐01’`
*   Packed data values: If you need to conserve storage space, use the `scale_factor` and `add_offset` attributes ([Section 3.1.1](#c03.xhtml_c3-sec-0003)), so that the packed data can be stored as a byte, short or integer and then read in using:

    ```
     unpacked_data = packed_data * scale_factor + add_offset
    ```

## 10.6 Summary

This chapter provides an overview of some of the available formats that you can encode your data in, such as text, pickle, and netCDF, using common packages. Good archiving habits should not be saved for the end of the project, when you are likely pressured by deadlines, but rather be a continuous component of development. For one, projects can quickly grow bigger than we expect. For example, when making plot titles I recommend extracting the relevant date, variable, and satellite name from the filename or metadata data, not by typing this information into your code by hand. If you reuse the code later, you do not have to manually update the title. Second, deadlines will tempt you to quickly finish up the project, and the data can be cryptic to interpret later. Taking the extra time today to curate your data will save you time in the future. In a community sense, doing so makes it easier to share your results with others.

At this point, you have learned many fundamental coding techniques using Python that are relevant to working with satellite data. In the next section, I will discuss how to integrate Python into a larger workflow. The concept of workflow goes beyond the syntax of how to perform a task to address how we can manage and organize our projects as whole.

# Part III
Effective Coding Practices

# 11
DEVELOPING A WORKFLOW

> Cultivating a community‐minded a code environment, practice, and style can improve programming efficiency for the scientist and make code easier to share. This chapter will describe some tools to increase productivity and strategies to improve your code, such as scripting, using version control, and virtual environments. The chapter also provides an overview of how to plan and develop code in an agile framework.

Once you have gained fluency in working with Earth observations in Python, you will likely look for ways to make your code more efficient, both in terms of code readability and computational performance. In this chapter, I will show some (1) tools to increase your productivity and (2) strategies to improve your code. This is encompassed by the term **workflow**. In context of this book, here I define workflow as *the common coding environment, practices, and style used to ensure programs and data are reusable and sharable in a scientific community and workplace*. Note the phrase “in a scientific community and workplace.” Workflows are not universal, but rather, arise from an individual or a group’s needs and experiences. Workflows can also evolve as needs within a community change and new technology becomes available.

From my personal experience, I have found it valuable to strategize before writing or updating code. In the past, I would often write code as fast as possible, get the result I needed, and move on to another task. I did not back‐up or document my code and I did not write the code in a general way so that it would be easier to update the next time. My functions were highly interconnected with and dependent on other functions. Over time, my code became increasingly complex and more difficult to update. I adopted better habits after I saw that was spending more time debugging my existing code than if I started over. As my career progressed, I often received code from colleagues, which at times was decades old. In both cases, It was clear which code was carefully planned and which code was hastily written to meet deadlines. In both cases, I learned that code may exist in a community far longer than the author anticipated.

I hope by sharing my story you are motivated to adopt good coding tools and techniques. Beginning in this chapter, I will touch on more advanced topics such as:

*   Creating scripts from notebooks to automate tasks
*   Saving changes to your code with version control
*   Using virtual environments so that code is reproducible
*   Managing your coding projects using agile principles

These techniques are not meant to stifle you, but rather to create a system where you can continue to grow as a programmer and establish good coding habits early on. While your workflow may not be the exact same as mine, many of the tools and concepts covered here are relevant to academic, industry, and government communities.

## 11.1 Scripting with Python

Jupyter Notebook is an excellent tool when you are new to Python. While you were leaning, I encouraged you to code in notebooks because:

*   Inline plots are helpful for quickly viewing your work.
*   Code snippets must be complete and fully executable, meaning you must think ahead about the entire purpose of the code block.
*   You can document your code inside the notebook and within the code block itself.

However, notebooks also have some drawbacks, especially once your projects increase in complexity:

*   They can produce unexpected results if the cells are run out of order.
*   Notebooks can be difficult to compare using version control and it is not easy to import one notebook within another.
*   Using Jupyter Notebook on a remote server is difficult for novices (see Appendix B.2 for instructions).
*   To run scripts, you must launch Jupyter Notebook and either select “run all” from the menu or manually run each cell in the notebook.

For me, the last point is the primary reason I turn to **scripting**. For example, generating 100 different images of Hurricane Harvey might be tedious to run in Jupyter Notebook. Instead, I would create a script, which is a collection of complete code that can be executed automatically to complete a task. To run the code, I would execute my script in the **terminal** (also called a **command line**), which is a text‐based application for manipulating files on your computer. After entered, the computer interprets the script commands using the **shell**, which is the interface between the text commands and the operating system. Every operating system has a shell, which include Bourne again shell (bash) on Unix/Linux and cmd or powershell on Windows.

All the examples in this chapter use basic commands in the bash syntax. If you are brand new to the command line or shell scripting, I recommend checking out The Command Line lessons on Ryan’s Tutorials website ([https://ryanstutorials.net/linuxtutorial/commandline.php](https://ryanstutorials.net/linuxtutorial/commandline.php)).

### 11.1.1 Creating Scripts Using Text Editors

Python scripts are often written in a **text editor**, which is a program that allows you to edit text while adhering to a standard encoding format, such as UTF‐8 (Section 5.1). Text editors are different from **word processors**, which support stylized text (such as special formatting like bold and italic as well as various fonts and size options). Because of this, word processors can introduce unprinted characters that will not be recognized by the Python interpreter when it is run.

Most operating systems come with a basic text editor. These are TextEdit on Mac, Notepad on Windows, or vi/vim on Linux. You can use your native text editor, but I recommend downloading and installing the popular Sublime Text editor ([https://www.sublimetext.com/](https://www.sublimetext.com/)) or my personal favorite, Atom ([https://atom.io/](https://atom.io/)). Text editors are designed to write code: they make it easy to navigate code, allow you to find and replace variables, and apply color schemes to Python syntax so that it’s more readable. **Integrated Development Environments** (IDE) have many of the same features as text editors but may also allow you to integrate with the command line to run and debug you code. Jupyter Notebook is an example of an IDE that uses the web browser to develop code. I also highly recommend PyCharm ([https://www.jetbrains.com/pycharm/](https://www.jetbrains.com/pycharm/)). I have a list of both text editors and IDEs in Appendix D.1 if you want to explore more popular options. For this section, I will discuss the steps using a text editor.

Open your text editor of choice and create a new file with a `.py` extension. Let’s create a very simple script called `hello_earth.py` to get started. In this script, I rewrite the “Hello Earth” example from [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml):

```
 #!/usr/bin/env python3

print("Hello Earth") 
```

One difference from Jupyter Notebook is that there is a **shebang** at the top of the file. The shebang tells the operating system which interpreter to use – in this case, python.

### 11.1.2 Creating Scripts from Jupyter Notebook

One of the simplest ways to create a script is to convert an existing Jupyter Notebook. As an example, I created a notebook named `script_example` that only contains one line of code: `print(“Hello Earth”)` . I can convert any Jupyter Notebook to a script by going to File *→* Download as *→* Python (.py), as shown in [Figure 11.1](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0001).

This will download a new file ( `script_example.py` ) to your computer. If you open the file using your text editor, you will see:

```
 #!/usr/bin/env python3
# coding: utf-8

# In[1]:

print("Hello Earth")
```

![Schematic illustration of converting Jupyter Notebook to a script.](images/c11f001.jpg)

[**Figure 11.1**](#c11.xhtml_R_c11-fig-0001) Converting Jupyter Notebook to a script.

You will notice that the script contains the line numbers ( `ln[1]` ), which in my opinion is unnecessary and should be removed from your script. There are ways to turn this behavior off in Jupyter Notebooks, which I show in Appendix B.3.

### 11.1.3 Running Python Scripts from the Command Line

Now I am finished editing the code and I want to run it. I will discuss two ways you can run Python scripts: one using the command line interpreter and the other using **iPython** ([Figure 11.2](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0002)). iPython is an interactive command line that allows you to run code in chunks. In fact, Jupyter Notebook is built using iPython, which explains the similarity in behavior.

For both options, I will need to open the command line. On Windows, I suggest using the Anaconda Prompt, which you can access from the start menu or using Anaconda Navigator. On Mac or Linux, open the Terminal app. Once the command line is open, you start in a default location. For example, I am using Windows, and when I launch the Anaconda Prompt, I see:

```
 (base) C:\Users\rebekah>
```

Now, I want to navigate to where our script is. To do this, I will change directories using `cd` . For example, if your code is stored in `C:\Documents\Python` , you can type:

```
 cd C:\Users\rebekah\Documents\Python
```

The command line will now be updated showing:

```
 (base) C:\Users\rebekah\Documents\Python>
```

![Schematic illustration of a flow chart depicts the steps necessary to run scripts in the command line.](images/c11f002.gif)

[**Figure 11.2**](#c11.xhtml_R_c11-fig-0002) A flow chart of the steps necessary to run scripts in the command line.

Now that I am in the right place, I can call the **Python interpreter**, which converts your code into a format that your computer can understand and executes the command. If you installed Anaconda, this includes a Python interpreter than you can use ( `python3` ). So, to run the script, type:

```
 python3 hello_world.py
```

If successful, “Hello Earth” should print to your screen.

A second method is to use `iPython` , which allows you to open Python in interactive mode. Unlike the command line method, iPython will let you run code line‐by‐line. So, like Jupyter Notebook, you have the option to copy and paste you code from the text editor in chunks into the iPython window. You can also call the entire script inside iPython. This is done by starting iPython and using the command `%run [script name].py` . Below is a capture from my terminal:

```
 Python 3.7.6 (default, Jan 8 2020, 20:23:39) [MSC v.1916 64 bit (AMD64)]
Type 'copyright', 'credits' or 'license' for more information
IPython 7.12.0 -- An enhanced Interactive Python. Type '?' for help.

In [1]: %run script_example.ipynb
Hello Earth
```

One advantage of using iPython is that after the script finishes running, variables that were generated in the script are still in memory. Then, you can print or operate on the variables to either debug or to develop your code further.

You may have noted two differences in workflow for write code in scripts versus notebooks, (1) that code cannot be inline and (2) the program must run fully to the end. Your code will certainly be more complex than this example. No matter your level of expertise, it is unlikely that you will code your program perfectly without any errors on the first try. To help avoid errors, text editors and IDEs will directly interface with the command line so that you can run your code inside the editor. Furthermore, some text editors and IDEs offer syntax checkers, such as vim flake8, PyCharm, and Sublime text ([Section 12.1.2](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0003)). Another method of debugging is to continue testing code in notebooks, where you can run code line‐by‐line to check the output, and then export it to the script like I showed in [Section 11.1.2](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0004).

### 11.1.4 Handling Output When Scripting

In the previous example, I printed text to the screen, but Python is capable of saving figures and data. Recall from [Section 10.1](#c10.xhtml_c10-sec-0002), the plots can be saved to file using the `plt.savefig()` instead of `plt.show()` .

It is possible to directly display your graphics using the X11 protocol (by default in Linux) with XQuartz (Mac) or PuTTy (Windows). I typically discourage this because satellite imagery tends to be very large and thus slow to display remotely. From my experience, it is usually faster to write an image to a file and then view the plot after it is fully rendered. While I wanted to make you aware of this option, I won’t describe how to set this up in this book.

## 11.2 Version Control

**Version control** is a way of tracking changes to your code over time. When developing code, version control benefits you because you can easily roll back to an older version if you introduce a bug. If you are collaborating on code with others, version control helps keep track of the differences between the multiple versions. I recommend **Git** due to its popularity and because Git easily connects with **online repositories**, which can host your Python projects and documentation online. You can install Git with Xcode Command Line Tools on Mac OS and with Git for Windows (https://gitforwindows.org). Linux users can install Git using a package manager, such as apt, yum, and dnf. While Git is a powerful tool with many features, in this section I will only cover the basics.

First, using the command line ([Section 11.3.2](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0013)), you will initialize a **repository** for your code. This defines and labels which files are being tracked for changes. It is typically the parent directory of the project. In theory, you can add any number and kind of files to your repository. For example, you can add netCDF files to your repository, but it’s not likely that you need to track changes in these files. Furthermore, because the files are large relative to the size of your code, tracking changes in netCDF files in your repository will slow down the time it takes to save the changes. So, you want to be selective about what files you add to your repository.

From the command line, change your directory to your project using the `cd` command (e.g. `cd C:/Documents/python_projects` ) and type:

```
 git init
```

This starts Git in the current directory. If this is your first time running git, you will see a prompt asking you to type your name and email. For practice, I will create a text file with the words “Hello Earth” and name it `git_practice.txt` . Then create another empty file called `irrelevant.txt` .

Next, you will specify which files are tracked using `git add` . All other files are not tracked. For the example, I will add `git_practice.txt` :

```
 git add git_practice.txt
```

To check if the file was successfully added to the repository, type:

```
 git status
```

Similarly, to remove files from being tracked, use `git remove filename` . For example, if you add `irrelevant.txt` , you can remove it from your repository using `git remove irrelevant.txt` . This does not delete the file from your repository, but Git will no longer track the changes.

If you make small changes and want to save the latest version of the code but not any of the intermediate steps, use the `git add` command again to update the file. Once you make a major change, the code is in a state that you want to make a permanent checkpoint. You can return to at a later time if you `commit` the change. This will give you a specific code version to return to, which includes a hash identification number specifically pointing this version.

To save them to the repository, use the `git commit` command to either update an individual tracked files. The `‐m` option will add a commit message that will detail what the changes were.

```
 git commit [filename] -m ‘initialize the project repository’
```

Adding commit messages is mandatory in Git. If you do not add the `‐m` option, a text editor screen will appear, prompting you to add a message regarding what has been changed in the code. A good commit message has a clear and short subject line but can contain a more details in the body of the message. Stylistically, it’s recommended that the subject complete the sentence “If applied, this commit will _____” in 50 characters or less. I will discuss this more in [Section 12.1.1](#c12.xhtml_c12-sec-0002).

You can now continue to make changes to the code, and when ready to save your current version of the project, use the `commit` command. There are many useful cheat sheets for git version control available online.

### 11.2.1 Code Sharing though Online Repositories

Online code repositories are websites that can host code. While many of them are for‐profit entities, others offer free services. The benefits of using these commercial services is that the code is not linked to a particular institution. Therefore, if you change your affiliation you can continue to access and control your code. Additionally, these platforms are highly searchable and thus provide access to a broader user community outside of the science community. The online repositories also respect licensing, accessibility, and the spirit of open‐source code.

In the next section, I show a short tutorial on setting up the GitHub, but the steps are not significantly different if you decide to use other platforms. Other platforms include Bitbucket and GitLab. Both GitHub and Bitbucket are free to use but are commercially owned by Microsoft Corporation and Atlassian Inc. GitLab is open source, but the set‐up process is a little more advanced.

### 11.2.2 Setting up on GitHub

Follow the steps below on the GitHub website:

*   Go to [www.github.com](http://www.github.com/) and set up an account. Log into your account.
*   Create a new online repository: go to your GitHub page and click on the + button and select “Create new project.”
*   Pick a name for the repository and add an optional short description.

Now, switch back to your command line. GitHub provides the exact code for you to copy and paste into your command line.

If you have new project and have not initialized your project:

```
 echo "# test" >> README.md git init
git add README.md
git commit -m "first commit"
```

If you created a repository in the previous section, you can add to it using the following code:

```
 git remote add origin https://github.com/[username]/[repository].git git push -u origin main
```

If successful, you should be able to refresh the webpage and see your code in the online repository. If you have a README.md or LICENSE.txt file in your repository, these will automatically be added to your repository.

When you commit, the changes stay local. Every so often, use `git push origin main` to upload the code online to the repository. This allows you to navigate through previous commits, older versions of the code, and share with other group members. If anything happens on your computer or server, your code will be backed up online. While you can do all of these tasks via the command line, I most often rely on my GitHub page to access my projects and share them with others.

## 11.3 Virtual Environments

Anaconda comes with over 250 installed packages, and you can import any (or all) of these packages while working in the default `base` environment. However, in practice most projects should only require a small subset of these packages. So instead of working in the `base` environment, I can create a **virtual environment**, which is a self‐contained installation of Python that only installs the necessary packages for a specific code or project.

Keeping separate virtual environments between projects is useful because it (1) mitigates the impact that package conflicts has on your anaconda installation (2) ensures that you and your colleagues can re‐run the code using exact same packages and versions in the future. Package conflicts arise because packages often depend on other packages, but they may have a mismatch in the dependency package version. If `conda` detects a mismatch, `conda` will ask you to update or downgrade the version, which could cause unexpected behavior if the versions are significantly different. If you encounter significant problems and bugs because of package conflicts, it is a lot easier to deactivate and recreate an environment than to reinstall Anaconda. Furthermore, environments also make it easier to distribute code, because you can communicate the exact packages and versions you used to get your results.

While this book uses Python 3, you may come across code written in Python 2.7\. **I strongly discourage you from writing any new code in Python 2.7**, as it is no longer being maintained. However, occasionally there are reasons for working with or updating Python 2.7 code rather than updating to Python 3\. In these rare cases, you can create a new environment that installs Python 2.7 in an isolated manner. As you become more sophisticated with your code, I suggest you also learn how to work with different environments.

I will describe how to change environments using `conda` . Keep in mind that that there are a number of other tools to manage your environments (such as [pyenv](https://github.com/pyenv/pyenv)).

Working with environments requires three steps:

1.  Create the environment.
2.  Activate the environment to enter it.
3.  Deactivate to leave the environment.

Step one is by far the most complicated because you must communicate to the new environment which packages you want to install, optionally including their version numbers. Below, I will start with the simplest case and build complexity.

### 11.3.1 Creating an Environment

If you prefer using a GUI, you can create a new environment inside the Anaconda Navigator. Anaconda provides excellent tutorials on package management ([https://docs.anaconda.com/anaconda/navigator/tutorials/manage‐packages/](https://docs.anaconda.com/anaconda/navigator/tutorials/manage-packages/)), so I encourage you to visit there if you are uncomfortable using the command line.

The same steps can also be completed from the command line, e.g. Anaconda Prompt on Windows, Terminal on Mac/Linux. If you used the Anaconda 3 installer that I recommended in Appendix A.1, the command below will create a new installation of Python 3:

```
 conda create --name pyviz
```

The result is a clean environment that I named `pyviz` . When I say clean, I mean that this environment will, by default, not have access to any packages. If, for example, you want to import NumPy in your code, you must explicitly pass a list of packages that want to be installed in this environment. For example, to install NumPy, type:

```
 conda create -n pyviz numpy==1.17.2
```

Most likely, you will need to install many packages and it is inconvenient to type a long list. For instance, you may want to install Pandas, netCDF4, scipy, and xarray in addition to NumPy. Fortunately, you can write your complete list of packages and versions out in a text file:

```
 netCDF4==1.5.2
numpy==1.17.2 
pandas==0.25.1 
scipy==1.3.1 
xarray==0.13.0
```

Then, instead of writing the individual packages, pass this text file using the `‐‐file` argument:

```
 conda create --name pyviz --file requirements.txt 
```

Then `conda` will search the channels and install the packages you explicitly specified (Appendix A.2). However, one or more package might not be available in your channels. To see what channels are being searched in conda, type:

```
 conda config --show channels
```

Some packages are only available on `conda‐forge` . You can add `conda‐forge` to the list of channels that `conda` searches when you create an environment using:

```
 conda config --append channels conda-forge
```

If you want to further streamline this process, you can create a YAML Ain’t Markup Language (YAML) file that includes the environment name, which channels you need to search, and a complete list of your requisite packages and versions. YAML is not a different filetype, but rather a text formatting framework that is used by a number of different coding languages, particularly for configuration files.

You can open your text editor and write out the packages you need. Below is an example of how the file is formatted:

```
 name: pyviz 
channels:
- conda-forge
- defaults 
dependencies:
- python=3.7
- cartopy
- dask
- matplotlib
- netcdf4
- h5py
- numpy
- pandas
- proj4
- xarray
- ipykernel
- notebook
- nb_conda_kernels
```

YAML files are case and space sensitive. The first line names the environment ( `name` ), which helps to distinguish multiple environments. The list following `channels` provides a list of package repositories to search. The `dependencies` include a list of packages. Note that you must include `notebook` (for Jupyter Notebook) or ipython ( `ipykernel` ) if you want to use either tool in the new environment. Otherwise, you will only be able to run Python scripts.

The above example or something similar can be saved to a file called `environment.yml` . When you create your environment, point to the YAML file (rather than typing each package) to import all libraries at once:

```
 conda env create -f environment.yml
```

### 11.3.2 Changing Environments from the Command Line

Once you have a complete list of your requisite libraries, activate the environment:

```
 conda activate pyviz
```

To see if you were successful, the command below will list all available environments with an asterisk next to the active one:

```
 conda info --envs
```

You may also want to save a current set of installed packages used to run your code. To create a list of your current environment’s dependencies into a YAML file, type:

```
 conda env export > environment.yml
```

To exit your current environment, use the deactivate command. Note that this does not destroy the environment; you can switch back into at a later time if you wish.

```
 conda deactivate
```

If you are truly finished with an environment, you can get rid of it completely using `conda remove` (it will not be recoverable, unless you created a YAML file):

```
 conda remove --name myenv --all
```

### 11.3.3 Changing Environments in Jupyter Notebook

After activating the environment (pyviz), when you launch Jupyter Notebook it will use the packages in the new environment and not those in your base environment. However, Jupyter Notebook will not change into the new environment in an existing session, or if you launched it using Anaconda Navigator or a Jupyter Notebook shortcut on your desktop. To switch between environments in an existing Jupyter session, you can install an extension to Jupyter Notebook called `nb_conda` :

```
 conda install nb_conda
```

Restart Jupyter Notebook, and when the browser launches, you will see a tab called `conda` ([Figure 11.3](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0003)). Clicking it will reveal any environments you created in the command line and allow you to make new ones using the [+] icon. Note that you will need Jupyter installed in the environment, or it will not be available for you to select.

Then activate the above environment. If you have an existing notebook and you wish to switch environments, use the menus to navigate to the environment, which is shown in [Figure 11.4](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0004)

```
 Kernel → Change kernel → [environment name]. 
```

![Schematic illustration of installing nb_conda adds a new tab to Jupyter Notebook, which allows you to switch environments.](images/c11f003.jpg)

[**Figure 11.3**](#c11.xhtml_R_c11-fig-0003) Installing nb_conda adds a new tab to Jupyter Notebook, which allows you to switch environments.

Source: Project Jupyter.

![Schematic illustration of changing kernel inside Juypter Notebooks.](images/c11f004.jpg)

[**Figure 11.4**](#c11.xhtml_R_c11-fig-0004) Changing kernel inside Juypter Notebooks.

## 11.4 Methods for Code Development

To improve code quality, scientific programmers can borrow some fundamental techniques from software programmers, such as development methods, requirements and style specification, and debugging. This section provides an overview of a few code development methods you may want to consider.

![Schematic illustration of the comparison of waterfall and agile framework.](images/c11f005.gif)

[**Figure 11.5**](#c11.xhtml_R_c11-fig-0005) Comparison of waterfall and agile framework.

When we begin learning to code, we often employ what is known as a **waterfall method** to design our project ([Figure 11.5](#c11.xhtml_c11-fig-0005)). Before we start programming, we may plan a rough outline of the coding steps we need to take to complete our project. In the waterfall method, you work on these steps sequentially. For example, let’s say you want to create and save an RGB plot ([Section 9.4](#c09.xhtml_c9-sec-0007)) for a seminar you are giving in a week. You may plan to import ABI channels 2 and 3 (step 1), then regrid channel 2 to match channel 3 (step 2), and combine them using the NDVI formula (step 3) to create plot showing NDVI (step 4), and finish by saving the plot to file (step 5). For small projects, the waterfall method may work fine. After further consideration, you decide to generate an animation showing the NDVI changes over several years instead of single plot. However, in this more complex plan you may have trouble completing one of the upstream step. Since the code is longer, you may have trouble debugging your code. The risk of using the waterfall method is that since each step depends on a prior one, if one fails you will not have any plots for your seminar.

For complex projects, I recommend using the **agile** or **lifecycle** method of code development. The agile method was advanced by Silicon Valley programmers to rapidly build sophisticated software. The agile method breaks down the coding process into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. The agile method also encourages iteration: you add additional complexity to the program each time you work on it. As a first iteration using the earlier example, you could import a single file, create a single NDVI plot, and save it to file with a simple filename, such as NDVI_test.png. In the second iteration, you can improve the aesthetics of the figure by adding a color bar and axes labels, and then the figure save with a detailed filename that includes the date and scan times of the image which are pulled from the metadata in the file. In the third iteration, you might create a loop to plot *t,* adjust the color scales and create a detailed title, and format the positioning of the colorbar.

This method is advantageous if you need to immediately present your results before you are ready, since you will have something available to show. Second, it is easier to debug code if you test it as you go rather than inspecting the result. As mentioned, projects can easily increase in scope over time. You might discover that you do not actually need all those extra details that you originally planned. Additionally, the needs of the consumer of your image may have changed – maybe your advisor or boss wants to examine a different region or add in another dataset. Under the waterfall method, the code quickly become too complex to easily make these changes. Using the agile approach, it may be easier adjust your goals in the middle of the task.

In my discussion above, the agile method is more of a philosophical approach to coding than an actual coding technique. In practice, you will likely use both waterfall and agile for simple and complex projects, respectively. In summary, advanced planning can significantly help with meeting deadlines and ensuring that code is upgradable, rather than hastily written to meet a short deadline.

## 11.5 Summary

This chapter covered some of the tools that will help you integrate Python into your daily work. You have seen how you can use scripts to automate tasks that you once had to manually run in notebooks and use virtual environments to ensure that the correct packages are installed even when the code is run on a different computer or server. Version control helps track what changes are made to the code over time and to keep copies of older versions of the code in case a new feature breaks the code. Many of these ideas were adopted from the software development community but they are helpful as the scope of our projects increase.

The techniques in this chapter also make it easier to share your code and results. In the next and last chapter of this text, I will discuss how topics such as code techniques, documentation, and licensing can help you share your expertise and results with your research community.
