# 12
REPRODUCIBLE AND SHAREABLE SCIENCE

> Sharing scientific code reduces barriers in scientific achievement. This chapter describes some techniques to adhere to best coding practices, such as ensuring clear variable and function names and writing modular and reusable code. To write clean code, Python has stylistic rules, which are known as Python Enhancement Proposals (PEP) 8, and tools such as PyLint and flake8 to help both programmers and scientists to follow conventions. Clear commenting and documentation should be practiced for each new program written or when scientist revises existing code.

While access to satellite datasets has grown, the tools to read, analyze, and visualize the data typically have remained hidden behind closed doors. This is a significant barrier to using satellite data and highlights the importance of code sharing to increase the accessibility to scientific data and knowledge. Therefore, the importance of expanding access to easy data portal searches to retrieve satellite data and the software tools used to analyze it cannot be overstated. Important coding principles set forth in this text that include persistent product identifiers, clear metadata, common vocabularies and file structure, and following community standards all contribute to the sharing of scientific knowledge. Adhering to free and open source principles is not limited to data and analysis housed in large agencies, but also applies to individual researchers in industry, government, and academia. If we all follow the guidelines as a community, free and open source science will ultimately increase the productivity and rate of discovery for the whole community.

What practitioners do will ultimately feed back into the improvement of the satellite datasets because often research drives the development of **operational** datasets that agencies produce; once data are in production, the users themselves provide feedback into the research. By sharing tools with other researchers, datasets are more fully utilized, and satellite data providers can improve existing datasets to address community needs and develop new ones as demand grows.

Skill sharing also includes both human and machine aspects. For scientists, human aspects relate to the accessibility and reusability of the data and code. This means clean and updatable code, clear and concise documentation, and making code available online are important needs. On the machine side, data should be “FAIR” – Findable, Accessible, Intercomparable, and Reusable (Wilkinson et al., [2016](#c12.xhtml_c12-bib-0011)). This means that code and data should have a persistent identifier (e.g., a DOI), clear and relevant metadata, follow community standards, and include a usage license.

## 12.1 Clean Coding Techniques

> *Always leave the campground cleaner than you found it*.
> 
> *– Rule from the Boy Scouts of America*

The quote above applies to campgrounds and code alike: if you repeatedly returning to the same code, make an improvement to it each time. This does not require you to perfect it, but this philosophy will allow you to make progress over time.

In the flip side, increasing clutter in your code leads to **technical debt**, where the code becomes increasingly complicated and difficult to update. Eventually, you must either invest significant time rewriting the code to reduce the complexity. In the worst case, you may have to abandon the code and start anew (in my analogy, you can think of this as bankruptcy).

Considering the “cost” of poor coding practices is appropriate because there can be actual economic and productivity loss because of technical debt. For example, Twitter was originally developed using an interpreted language called Ruby on Rails, which made it easier to develop code and update it quickly but at the expense of computational speed. As Twitter became more popular, the search performance slowed significantly because of this initial design choice. Twitter then had to make an expensive investment to rewrite its platform in Java to meet customer demand. If you take the time now to write good, clean code, you will save yourself time (and possibly money) in the future.

[Table 12.1](#c12.xhtml_c12-tbl-0001) shows some example of how bad habits can make code unusable, particularly in scientific code (SourceMaking.com has some great descriptions and interactive examples of bad coding practices [https://sourcemaking.com/refactoring/smells](https://sourcemaking.com/refactoring/smells)):

| Problem | Definition | Solution |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bloat | An accumulation of functions, methods, and dependencies that become so long and require so many parameters that they are difficult to work with. | Write functions that do one action well, which will limit the number of requires parameters. |
| Change prevention | Your code is difficult to update because a change in one function or part of your code requires many fixes throughout the program. This high level of interdependency is known **tight coupling**. | Write functions and code blocks that are general and independent of each other. |
| Cruft | Pointless, redundant, or unused code or comments in your program. | Use version control to track changes and delete obsolete code. |

[**Table 12.1**](#c12.xhtml_R_c12-tbl-0001) Examples of Code Problems and Solutions

According to Robert C. Martin’s canonical Clean Code textbook, “Clean code does what it’s supposed to do without breaking. It is easy to read, maintain, and extend. It functions without problems or defects and is written so that it’s easy for the next person to work with.” Below, I will highlight some beginner friendly principles for developing code:

*   **Variable names** should be nouns, not verbs. Avoid using single letters or generic words like *data* or *array*. At the same time, they should not contain too much detail. In the code block, define variables within sections where they are used. This contrasts with legacy programming languages like Fortran, that requires all variables to be defined at the beginning of the entire code block.
*   **Function names** should include verbs, such as make, write, open, calculate, etc. For example, the pretend function, *open_SST(filename)*, has a good, descriptive name for its function of opening a file and returning SST data. Functions should be designed to do one thing well – so if you find yourself needing two verbs for the same function, it may be better to break the function into two parts. For example, `open_and_regrid_SST()` could be split into smaller, more digestible functions, `open_SST()` and `regrid_SST().` When defining functions, it is good to keep similar and dependent functions together in your code.
*   **Aesthetics**, such as how you group and space lines within your code, make your program easier to update. Group together the lines of code that illustrate a concept or are related. Conversely, separate different concepts with a new line. Every line of code should be short and digestible.

Things to avoid:

*   Rigid code that is difficult to change
*   Fragile code that is susceptible to bugs and crashes
*   Code that is not reusable for other tasks
*   Repetition in the code
*   Overly complex code
*   Code that is hard to understand

In practice, it is unlikely you will write clean and efficient code every single time you set out to write a program. Seeking perfection is stifling. This is where the agile method can help: on my first iteration, my code is simple and manageable while performing the intended task. I then add incremental complexity with each iteration. As my code becomes more complex, I also perform **refactoring**, which is when we restructure code to improve in both the function and cleanliness of the code. Each time I return to my code “campground,” I consider the principles in this section and ask myself if I can improve my code.

### 12.1.1 Stylistic Conventions

Python enhancement proposals (PEPs) consist primarily of documentation that explains the rational for new features, but also provides guidance to the community on how to stylize code. Of these, the stylistic rules in [PEP 8](https://www.python.org/dev/peps/pep-0008/) are the ones that the Python community follows closely. The clean coding principles apply, but PEP 8 provides added details such as using underscores to show spaces in variable names, all caps to show constants, and camel case to indicate a class. These style suggestions are aesthetic but also adds to code consistency.

### 12.1.2 Tools for Clean Code

Since there are many stylistic rules, it can be difficult to remember all of them, but there are several tools to perform **linting**, or ensuring that the PEP 8 style is followed. Below are two of these tools you can install:

*   **PyLint**
*   **flake8**

Both tools perform stylistic linting, where it checks to ensure the code follows the defined PEP eight conventions. Additionally, these tools can perform logical linting, which checks for code errors, code with unintended consequences, or patterns in your code that can lead to crashes.

## 12.2 Documentation

When you find yourself repeatedly using and updating the same piece of code, it means that your code is a solution to some problem. Thus, other researchers can benefit from your code and there will be value in sharing it. This requires properly documenting your code before distributing it to save other code users time and energy and to make your code transparent and understandable. Occasionally, we ourselves are the end user – I have many times returned to old code only to find that I have forgotten how to use it. There are two methods of documenting your code: external to the code using a README file, or inside the code through commenting. Different workplaces and communities (both scientific and open‐source projects) will have their own individual cultural expectations for what should be documented inside a code block. This section describes some of the standard documentation practices that are universal to the Python community.

### 12.2.1 Comments and Docstrings

One way of documenting code is using **document strings** (“docstrings”), which begin and end with quotes ( `"""` ) or ('''). The first statement in all functions, classes, and modules should include a docstring stating at minimum what the code does. A single line docstring is enough for simple functions. For more complex functions, the docstring should additionally contain the input arguments and the values returned ([PEP257](https://www.python.org/dev/peps/pep-0257/); Slatkin, 2015). The closing quotes for the docstring should be alone on the final line.

**Comments** occur within the body of code or functions. Ideally, comments should only be used to *clarify* code or *warn of consequences* (Martin, 2009). For science code, this may also cite the source of an empirical equation or constants. Python is a “readable” language ([PEP20](https://www.python.org/dev/peps/pep-0020/)), and the comments should in turn also be readable – use complete sentences and uses proper capitalization and punctuation.

**Inline comments**, which are comments on the same line as the code, are generally discouraged:

```
 List = [“Himawari”, “GOES”, “Meteosat”] # Geostationary satellites
```

In the scientific community, function arguments, outputs, and dependencies are often included at the top of the function definition. For example, for the haversine function in [Section 9.5.1](#c09.xhtml_c9-sec-0013):

```
 def haversine(deglat1, deglon1, deglat2, deglon2):
"""Calculates the distance in km between two geolocated points.

Arguments:
 deglat1, deglon1: the latitude and longitude coordinate point 1
 deglat2, deglon2: the latitude and longitude coordinate point 2

Returns:
 A float of the distance in km between the two points.
"""
 lat1=deglat1*np.pi/180.0
 lat2=deglat2*np.pi/180.0

 long1=deglon1*np.pi/180.0
 long2=deglon2*np.pi/180.0

 # The code below uses the haversine formula. 
 a = np.sin(0.5 * (lat2 - lat1))
 b = np.sin(0.5 * (long2 - long1))

 # Diameter of the Earth is 12742 km.
 dist = 12742.0 * np.arcsin(np.sqrt(a * a + np.cos(lat1) * np.cos(lat2) * b * b))

 return dist
```

Too many comments are also not helpful. As an example, the comment below is redundant and should be removed since the code command clearly explains what is happening:

```
 # Imports packages 
import numpy as np
```

I discourage commenting out unused code because that makes the code difficult to read. Instead, use version control ([Section 11.2](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0010)) to track changes to the code over time.

Lastly, comments that contradict or are outdated compare to the code below can confuse future users of the code. So, update all comments along with the code it describes.

### 12.2.2 README File

When I receive a new piece of code, I begin by first understanding how to run it. Therefore, a well‐written README file will enhance the functionality of your code with a clear explanation of how it is run. README files contain information on how to install and run code or software and also the intended goals.

While there is no standard structure, I suggest that a good README file should include the following sections, where applicable:

*   Title: A detailed name for your project.
*   Getting started: information on where to access the program, any relevant background literature, and external software dependencies.
*   Installation process: You can explicitly assume some basic familiarity with software tools (e.g., that they have Python) on the user’s part. If there are any external tools, then mention them here.
*   Required libraries: You can explicitly list them or point to any requirements or environment files that are included in the distribution.
*   How to use or run the code: This is a helpful guide for others using your code, or for yourself when returning to the code at another time.
*   Author(s): You and any other contributors to the project.
*   Resources or references: Any materials used to develop the code or further resources on your project.

There is no definitive template for a readme file; however, the above is a good start. Through this book, you should have collected a portfolio of practice projects. Practice creating a README file for one of these.

### 12.2.3 Creating Useful Commit Messages

As mentioned in [Section 11.2](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0010), commit message are mandatory in Git, and it is worth putting some thought into these messages. These commits provide a history for changes to the code. Over time, the code will become increasingly complex and you will not be able to visually find the differences. A good commit message should help the user understand what changes were made to the code without having to compare versions of the code.

A useful commit message has these features:

*   It will complete the following sentence: Applying this commit will . . .
*   It is less than 50 characters; longer messages will be cutoff on most websites.
*   Minor changes or additional details are in the body of the commit message.

The **SciPy** community (which develops **NumPy, Matplotlib,** and **Pandas**) uses additional recommendations for writing good commit recommendations. The messages should help the user understand what changes were made to the code without having to compare versions of the code. They also recommend starting a commit using a standard set of acronyms, such as:

```
 BUG: bug fix
DEP: deprecate something, or remove a deprecated object
DEV: development tool or utility
DOC: documentation
ENH: enhancement
MAINT: maintenance commit (refactoring, typos, etc.)
REV: revert an earlier commit
STY: style fix (whitespace, PEP8)
TST: addition or modification of tests
```

So, a useful commit message following the SciPy standards may look like the following:

```
 ENH: Improve reader to import full disc ABI data from all channels

The previous version of the code only could import ABI channel 6,
which was hardcoded into the import_ABI function. The function
has been rewritten to be more generalized, including:
* Handle data in mode 3, 4, and 6
* Check the filename to make sure the data are not mesoscale
```

## 12.3 Licensing

The author of creative work has exclusive rights to their product, or **implied exclusivity**. When you create a painting or write a book, the expectation is that you will be credited for you work. Code also falls under a “creative work” category. For instance, if you find code online, it is assumed that you will credit the author for their contribution. Technically speaking, you should not copy, distribute, or modify any code without explicit permission. However, coders should also be clear on their expectations regarding how the code is shared, used, and credited by choosing a software license for their work. There are several free and open‐source software (FOSS) licenses, some of the most common are listed below:

*   **Public domain.** The code is no longer owned by the creator, because it now belongs to the public. Credits to you will not be required.
*   **MIT/X11 licensing.** This is the most popular *permissive licensing* and one of the least restrictive in terms of use. People may use, copy, modify, distribute, or sell copies of your code. However, the code will credit the original author in all redistributions of the code. Permissive licenses like the MIT/X11 license have the few rules so they are also the easiest to administer.
*   **Apache 2.0.** Like the MIT license, except that it also requires the user to explicitly list out all the modifications that you have done in the original software, i.e., you are required to preserve the integrity of the original. Like MIT, it is a *permissive license*.
*   **GNU public license (GPL).** This license guarantees that your code will be perpetually open source. As a result, GPL is recommended for peer review and for encouraging community contributions. GPL permits commercial modified and distributed, but the new distribution is automatically open source. However, commercial developers can charge for technical support, customer‐requested updates, and consultation (Morin, 2012). Because GPL licenses are more restrictive than the other two, it is not considered a permissive license, but rather is considered a strong [copyleft](https://www.gnu.org/copyleft/) license. Copyleft licenses grant permission to use the code while maintaining the original license terms of the work and acknowledging the original authors in all derivative work.

Some licenses can be mixed and matched; for instance, GPL licensed code can be used in a project with MIT licensing. However, the reverse is not true. Any MIT licensed code containing GPL code is automatically GPL, since the latter is more restrictive. Note that aside from public domain, all the above licenses also include a “use at your own risk” statement to waive your liability and the users right to a warranty.

So, which one do you choose? The MIT/X11 license is one of the most popular licenses, primarily because it is relatively clear and concise compared with many of the other licenses. However, if you are concerned with the code becoming closed source or monetized by commercial entities, then opt for the GPL license. If you are still unsure, the [https://choosealicense.com](https://choosealicense.com/) website provides a user‐friendly template for selecting a license. Additionally, Morin et al. ([2012](#c12.xhtml_c12-bib-0007)) provide an excellent article describing the differences between the licensing options for science code.

Once you have selected your license, copy/paste the contents into a LICENSE.txt file in your code repository.

## 12.4 Effective Visuals

I encourage you to ask yourself the following question before start to make any figure: What are you trying to communicate, and how does it support your hypothesis? We want to think beyond simply making plots, but instead develop effective *visualizations*. A visualization is imagery, scientific plots, illustrations, or any other graphical representation of data. Once you learn to plot in Python, and especially once you learn to script, you can rapidly generate plots in a matter of seconds. While many figures are essential for seeing patterns, extrema, and trends, most will not effectively advance your hypothesis. So, what do effective visuals do?

### 12.4.1 Make a Statement

When artists begin a painting, they start with an empty canvas. Rather than working from corner to corner, an artist will likely paint the background first and gradually add layers of paint until the painting is complete. The matplotlib package starts with `plt.figure` , which initializes an empty canvas. Each line of code is like a layer of paint, adding axes, points, colors, lines, or annotations to the plot. The result is a complete figure.

However, just because a figure is successfully rendered does not make it a *meaningful* figure. Wilkinson, 2005 likens figures to words; when well written, words can communicate ideas and statements. Much like the layered approach described above, the process of designing a figure involves three stages:

1.  **Specification.** The components (or objects) that go into the plot. This includes the data, any data transformations, the choice of map projection, aesthetic attributes, legends, and color schemes. These are all the necessary components to make a visual.
2.  **Assembly.** The actual construction of the elements in the specification. Before aggregation, the items are standalone objects and do not inherit any properties from their parents.
3.  **Display.** The actual rendering following the assembly of the objects.

The above stages also permit easy revision. Changing the data from one plot type to another allows us to communicate different ideas about the underlying data.

### 12.4.2 Undergo Revision

It is easier than ever to create visualizations on computers ([Section 2.1](#c02.xhtml_c2-sec-0004)). Figures can quickly be rendered and then refined. In *Eloquent Science* (2009), David Schultz defines the steps that take a rough draft figure to a final one:

1.  **Design** as in the type and layout of the plot.
2.  **Size** of the final figure; vectorized graphics are easily scaled but may be very large for complex figures.
3.  **Aesthetics** that make the figure compact and self‐contained.
4.  **Consistency** within and between other figures.
5.  **Annotation** both within the figure and in the captions.

Creating a plot that satisfies these conditions produces a chart, perhaps even a visually pleasing one. However, in the next section, I will describe how to create meaningful plots.

### 12.4.3 Are Accessible and Ethical

Selecting appropriate labels, fonts, and colors for a plot is not trivial. Labels and fonts should be easy to read, and the figure should be self‐describing through text and graphical annotations. However, choosing colors requires you to consider what information you want to emphasize and make it easier for the reader to interpret. Rainbow is a common color scheme in Earth sciences, but I generally do not recommend using it. The reason is that the high‐perception colors create artificial patterns of information. Additionally, the human eye cannot accurately judge the magnitudes that are rainbow colored (Borland and Taylor, 2007). An example of the same plot of wildfire smoke plumes with two different color schemes can be seen in [Figure 12.1](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0001). The left image uses a two‐tone color scheme (purple-blue, or “PuBU”), while the image on the right uses many with sharp transition points (NCL’s default color). While the right image is useful for highlighting the smoke plume, you should be careful not to misrepresent results by using unnecessary high contrast to show patterns where they do not exist.

![Schematic illustration of an example of uniform color scheme (left) and how a different color scheme causes artificial enhancement of features (right) for the same image.](images/c12f001.jpg)

[**Figure 12.1**](#c12.xhtml_R_c12-fig-0001) Example of uniform color scheme (top) and how a different color scheme causes artificial enhancement of features (bottom) for the same image. Even though the data are using the same range (0, 35.0), the image on the right shows more structure. Both images show total column carbon dioxide (10^(18) molecules/cm²) from intense Australian wildfires during the afternoon orbit of Suomi NPP on 11 November 2019, from the NUCAPS algorithm. White colors are missing data caused by uniform clouds, precipitation, or gaps in the overpass.

Based on Smith et al., 2020.

![Schematic illustration of perceptually uniform sequential colors available in matplotlib.](images/c12f002.gif)

[**Figure 12.2**](#c12.xhtml_R_c12-fig-0002) Perceptually uniform sequential colors available in matplotlib.

Some color schemes are useful for quickly understanding certain types of data and analysis. As a result, some conventions have been informally adopted by research communities. Temperature anomaly data shows values that are above and below averages usually use blue for negative values, white for values near zero, and red for positive values. Alternatively, low and high rainfall plots often use brown (low values) and green (high values). There are no standard rules for choosing colors but consider what you are communicating to your audience (Rheingans, 2000).

In addition to the content, when you are choosing colors you should also consider human visual perception. Roughly 8% of males and 0.5% of females experience color blindness, the most common type is insensitivity to red and green (which is called *deuteranopia*). As a result, color schemes should avoid these two colors. Another visual challenge is contrast sensitivity, which peaks at age 20 and declines with age. High‐contrast color schemes make it easier for older age groups to distinguish lines. However, as mentioned, some high‐contrast color schemes can misrepresent the data. Perceptively uniform colormaps ([Figure 12.2](#c12.xhtml_c12-fig-0002)) address many of these (Kovesi, 2015). Most perceptively uniform colormaps are color‐blind friendly and high contrast, and many are grayscale printing friendly. While not a vision impairment, printing scientific papers and related figures is still a common practice around the world.

When designing figures, no one color scheme satisfies all requirements. Figures should be both truthful and inclusive when presenting information. Often, picking good colors involves trial and error, but there are some useful resources, such as Color Brewer 2.0 ([https://colorbrewer2.org/](https://colorbrewer2.org/)). Also, remember that colors are not the only way to distinguish plot elements. For instance, patterns and line styles (e.g., solid, dotted, dashed) are useful choices for making figures more accessible (and printer friendly). Additionally, you can also choose to spread out information in two simpler plots, rather than one more complex plot.

## 12.5 Summary

In this chapter, I provided an overview of some of the clean coding techniques and how to effectively document and license your code. When writing and documenting your code, I recommend that you consider the potential users within your research scientific community. This means the code should be clearly written and easy to update, and the supporting documentation should be descriptive. You should also consider the audience when making visualizations, such as using colorblind‐friendly colors and large text labels. Many scientists improve their coding skills through hands‐on experience, through mentorship by our colleagues, or self‐study. By providing your peers with helpful tools and resources, you can contribute to your community in a way that goes beyond to your scientific contribution.

# CONCLUSION

I hope you feel empowered to find relevant satellite data for your project and feel equipped with the tools to visualize it. If you have worked through the examples in this book, you should have basic fluency in Python as it applies to some common satellite datasets. In this text, I showed how to import a variety of common file formats, create basic plots, transform data to a variety of map projections, change the spatial gridding, and combine datasets from different sensors. This book is only an introduction to the vast amounts of satellite data, but these principles can be transferred to other data products. I recommend that you practice these skills using other observations. Appendix E has a tutorial on accessing NASA and NOAA data. You can access data from ESA ([https://earth.esa.int/eogateway/](https://earth.esa.int/eogateway/)) or through NOAA’s THREDDS data server: [https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/thredds/catalog.html](https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/thredds/catalog.html).

There are other books and resources you can access to hone your programming and analysis skills. For instance, *Python Programming and Visualization for Scientists* by Alex DeCaria provides a wonderful introduction to Python for new coders. If you are interested in machine learning, *Python Machine Learning* by Wei‐Meng Lee is an excellent gateway to advanced statistical analysis. The online book *Python for Data Science* ([https://jakevdp.github.io/PythonDataScienceHandbook/](https://jakevdp.github.io/PythonDataScienceHandbook/)) provides an overview of the basics and also emerging fields like machine learning. Appendix C has additional learning resources that can benefit you on your journey.

Please continue to hone your Python programming skills. At the time of this writing, there are over 4,000 books about Python on Amazon (so thank you for reading this one!) and many free and online tutorials. Although there are numerous written resources, reading and writing as much code as you feel comfortable will only increase your skill. I encourage you to share your struggles and successes (e.g., code, documentation, and tutorials) with the community so that others may learn, too. To ask questions, you can connect with an online community such as Pangeo ([https://discourse.pangeo.io/](https://discourse.pangeo.io/)). In the end, sharing your Python projects will benefit you because it will build interest in your work, and it will benefit others as well as you contribute to your field.

# Appendix A
INSTALLING PYTHON

You will need an internet connection to obtain Python and download the tutorials. If you do not have a persistent internet connection, I suggest following the entire setup tutorial below. Then, the remainder of the book can be completed offline.

If you are completely new to Python, you can also run the notes interactively online using Binder, which can be found on the book GitHub page ([https://github.com/resmaili/EarthSciViz](https://github.com/resmaili/EarthSciViz)). Binder sessions create an executable environment in your web browser to run the code in this book. However, once you wish to develop your own code, you will need to install Python on your workstation.

## A.1 Download Tutorials for This Book

All tutorials shown in this book, including both code and datasets, are accessible through [https://resmaili.github.io/Earth-Obs-Py](https://resmaili.github.io/Earth-Obs-Py). If you are unfamiliar with using git and GitHub, the tutorials can be downloaded to your computer by selecting Code ➔ Download ZIP (shown below).

![Snapshot of downloading tutorials for this book.](images/Appauf001.jpg)

## A.2 Download and Install Anaconda

Python is included on many Unix installations. However, I recommend that beginners install the Anaconda package manager. The Anaconda distribution includes standard Python, many scientific packages, and the Jupyter Notebook development environment that I use for examples in this book. Anaconda installs over 150 packages and is quite large (2 GB). If download speeds and space are a limitation for you, you can download Miniconda (400 MB), which contains fewer installed packages.

First, install the latest version of Anaconda (Python version 3.8 or newer, not version 2) appropriate to your operating system, [ww](http://www.anaconda.com/download/)w[.anaconda.com/download/](http://www.anaconda.com/download/) This step is operating‐system independent. Miniconda can be downloaded from [https://docs.conda.io/en/latest/miniconda.html](https://docs.conda.io/en/latest/miniconda.html).

## A.3 Package Management in Anaconda

Downloading and installing Anaconda provides you with many scientific **packages**, along with **development environments** such as Jupyter Notebook, iPython, and Spyder. However, you will need additional third‐party packages to be able to import, analyze, and plot satellite datasets that I show in this text. You can download and install packages from package **repository channels**, which are like online catalogs of python packages.

If you try to import a package that is not installed, Python returns a `ModuleNotFoundError` , which looks like:

```
 ModuleNotFoundErrorTraceback (most recent call last)
<ipython-input-1-d16bc6fdb2bf> in <module>
----> 1 import cartopy
ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'cartopy'
```

I show the steps on how to do this via the command line using `conda` ([https://conda.io/en/latest/](https://conda.io/en/latest/)), an open‐source package and environment ([Section 11.3](#c11.xhtml_c11-sec-0014)) management system. If you are unfamiliar with the command line, Anaconda comes with Anaconda Navigator ([https://docs.anaconda.com/anaconda/navigator/tutorials/manage‐packages/](https://docs.anaconda.com/anaconda/navigator/tutorials/manage-packages/)). Anaconda Navigator is a desktop graphical user interface (GUI) which may be more intuitive to new programmers. For more information on using it to install packages, see Anaconda Navigator's online user guide.

To install packages, you will need to access the terminal on Mac or Linux and the Anaconda Prompt on Windows.

```
 conda install [package name]
```

This will check the default conda channel (https://repo.anaconda.com/pkgs/) for the package you specified above. However, the default channel does not contain all possible packages. `conda‐forge` is another community channel that contains many libraries and more up‐to‐date versions than those on the default channel. Many of the packages used in this book are only available on `conda‐forge` . You can add `conda‐forge` to the list of channels to search using:

```
 conda config --append channels conda-forge
```

Since you have several packages to install to work through this book, installing them individually takes time. So instead, you can install all the packages by either downloading or creating the `pyviz.yml` file ([https://github.com/resmaili/EarthSciViz](https://github.com/resmaili/EarthSciViz)):

```
 conda install --file pyviz.yml
```

If successful, you shouldn’t experience any `ModuleNotFoundError` messages while working through this book.

# Appendix B
JUPYTER NOTEBOOK

## B.1 Running on a Local Machine (New Coders)

Both Windows and Unix users can open the Anaconda Navigator to launch Jupyter Notebook. I prefer to open the terminal on Mac or Linux Machines or open the Anaconda Prompt in Windows and type:

```
 jupyter notebook
```

Windows users can navigate to a Jupyter Notebook icon from the start menu. If it succeeds, a browser window will open and you will see the Jupyter Notebook main page. To create a new notebook, click on New --> Python 3\. A new tab will open with an empty notebook. Here, the tab is opened in Mozilla Firefox.

![Snapshot of the Jupyter Notebook main page.](images/Appbuf001.jpg)

In the annotated screen capture from Jupyter below, **A** shows the menu options. File contains tasks such as save/rename; edit allows you to add, copy, duplicate, and cut cells. Cell permits you to run code blocks within cells and set the cell type. The latter is useful for converting your notebooks to latex or html. The Kernel menu allows you to stop a command or the entire session if something goes wrong (e.g. you try to open too many files and your computer runs out of memory).

![Snapshot shows many of the commonly accessed menu commands as icon shortcuts.](images/Appbuf002.jpg)

**B** shows many of the commonly accessed menu commands as icon shortcuts. The icons, from left to right, are save, new cell, cut a cell, copy a cell, duplicate cell, move cell up, move cell down, run active cell, stop the cell, restart the kernel, and restart and run all.

**C** is where we type code. Once you type your code into the cell, you can click the run button or hit Shift and Enter to run from the keyboard. The output will display below.

In **D**, you can rename the current notebook. By default, it will be called Untitled; get in the habit of giving your notebooks descriptive names because it is easy to accumulate many notebooks over time.

**E** shows a dropdown menu for you to select cell type. The options include code (default), markdown, or raw. Markdown is a plain text formatting syntax, so it’s valuable for documenting your code. Markdown’s presence in Jupyter Notebook is part of the reason they are called notebooks; you can mix code and detailed notes and documentation. Raw cell types are just plain text; all spaces and tabs will be preserved in this format.

Finally, **F** shows the status of the kernel. While most notebooks are written in Python, Jupyter Notebook also supports over 100 programming languages, although only include Python by default. So, the top right corner indicates what language the kernel is in and the small dot to the right indicates the status. If code is running, the dot will be filled in and you must wait for the cell to finish running before another is processed.

You are all set up! So, return to [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml) and go through the “Hello Earth” example to test out the above functions.

## B.2 Running on a Remote Server (Advanced)

Many researchers work off remote Linux servers hosted by their employer or university, which are often command line only. Below I briefly outline how to access a remote instance of Jupyter Notebook on a workplace server. Note that I assume that you have a working knowledge of the command line and are familiar with how to use the Secure SHell (SSH) protocol to connect your local computer to a remote server. I also assume you know how to use a VPN (Virtual Private Network) client if it is required to connect to your server remotely. If you are not familiar with how to use VPN and SSH, you will need to check with your IT department about how to get set up.

There are two potential ways to run Jupyter Notebook remotely. First, you can launch a browser on your remote server (e.g., Mozilla Firefox) to run Jupyter Notebook. This means you enabled X11 Forwarding set‐up and log‐in using the `ssh ‐X ‐Y` flags. However, I do not recommend this work environment because I have experienced significant lags when displaying the web browser window, which is frustrating. The second method is using your own computer’s *local browser* to eliminate this lag. You will need to have Anaconda installed on your remote machine. I personally recommend installing your own copy of Anaconda – for instance, your home directory – rather than use a shared version. However, if your institution has other recommendations, follow their guidelines.

Before I proceed further, I will need to select a local **port number** and determine the remote server’s **hostname**. Port numbers are used to identify the endpoints in a line of communication between two machines. The value of the port number is somewhat arbitrary, although certain port numbers are reserved for certain tasks. Typically, 8000 or 8080 are usually available. However, you may need to confirm this with your IT department.

The hostname is a label used to identify which remote server you are connecting to. On the remote server command line, the server hostname address can be found using:

```
 hostname -I
```

Next, I need to set up an **SSH tunnel**, which connects content between your remote server and local browser. Using the hostname, type the following command to enable Jupyter Notebook:

```
 jupyter notebook --no-browser --port=[port number] --ip=[hostname]
```

To access the Jupyter Notebook, you can also copy and paste the URL that is generated following from the previous command.

```
 To access the notebook, open this file in a browser:
file:///C:/Users/rebekah/AppData/Roaming/jupyter/runtime/nbserver-18284-open.html

Or copy and paste one of these URLs: http://hostname:8080/?token=e5f68voes8wcz7bs7m0ssedouziikvk9z41liz6u
```

You can copy and paste this address into your local browser (e.g., Firefox, Chrome, Safari) and access notebooks on the remote server.

## B.3 Tips for Advanced Users

If you are comfortable working on a Linux machine, some of the following suggestions may be useful for you.

**Use an alias to establish the remote tunnel**. You can save the above tunnel command as an alias in your `.bashrc` or `.bash_profile` file. For example:

```
 jn=jupyter notebook --no-browser --port=[port number] --ip=[hostname]
export jn
```

**Use a windows manager to maintain a semi‐permanent tunnel.** Windows managers like `Screen` ([www.gnu.org/software/screen/](https://www.gnu.org/software/screen/)) or `tmux` ([https://github.com/tmux/tmux/wiki](https://github.com/tmux/tmux/wiki)) are useful in their own right, but you can also use them to create a session where you run your tunnel command (e.g., the one I saved to the alias `jn` ), and then detach the session. By doing this, your notebooks will continue to run in the background even if your current connection has dropped or timed out.

If you enable passwords (see the next section on configuration files), you do not need to type the hash each time you run Jupyter Notebook. You can also then bookmark the notebook location in your web browser.

### B.3.1 Customizing Notebooks with Configuration Files

Jupyter options customizable inside the configuration file ( `jupyter_notebook_config.py` ). The configuration file is in your home directory `(~/.jupyter` on Linux/Mac, or the `%APPDATA%` folder on Windows), unless you choose to install Anaconda in another location on your computer. By default, the configuration file will not exist. To generate the configuration file, open the terminal (Linux/Mac) or the Anaconda prompt (Windows) and type:

```
 jupyter notebook --generate-config
```

If you check, in your Jupyter directory you will see `jupyter_notebook_config.py` . Open `jupyter_notebook_config.py` with a text editor.

Some useful customizations include:

*   **Setting a password**

For security, the notebook URL will include a very long hash token, which, for example, looks like:

```
 http://hostname:8080/?token=e5f68voes8wcz7bs7m0ssedouziikvk9z41liz6u
```

If you leave off the token, you will be prompted for a password, which you will need to set up. The advantage of setting up a password is that every time you start a remote session of a notebook, the hash will change. By setting up a password, the location will stay the same (and you can bookmark it in your browser).

Uncomment the line by deleting a # symbol at the very beginning of the line `c.NotebookApp.allow_password_change` (see example below).

```
 # While logging in with a token, the notebook server UI will give the opportunity 
# to the user to enter a new password at the same time that will 
# replace the token login mechanism. This can be set to false to prevent changing password from the UI/API.
c.NotebookApp.allow_password_change = True
```

Then in the command line, set the password by typing:

```
 jupyter notebook password
```

You will be prompted to enter and re‐enter a password, which will generate a password in `jupyter_notebook_config.json.` Note that it is not encrypted, so for security, do not use a sensitive password. Then, you will not need the hash to access the Jupyter Notebook:

```
 http://hostname:8080/
```

Then, you will be prompted to enter a password.

*   **Suppressing Script numbers in Jupyter Notebook**

For advanced users, you can suppress this output by customizing your `jupyter_nbconvert_config.py` file ([https://nbconvert.readthedocs.io/en/latest/customizing.html](https://nbconvert.readthedocs.io/en/latest/customizing.html)).

### B.3.2 Starting and Ending Python Scripts

When writing a Python script, the first two lines of code are **encoding declarations,** which are used by the system before processing the code. The first line is called a **shebang**, which tells your system which interpreter to use when running the code. In this example below, I want to call python3 to run my code. This will line will call `python3` based on your `PATH` environment variables, which are set in the `.bash_profile` or `.bashrc` at startup. If your `PATH` points to your local Anaconda Python installation, this version will run instead of the version that is installed on your system. In the second encoding declaration line, there is a statement referring to the text encoding, which is UTF‐8\. UTF‐8 is the default, but you have the option of using other types.

```
 #!/usr/bin/env python3
# coding: utf-8

import sys
print("Hello Earth") 
sys.exit()
```

In the body of the code block above, I import the `sys` package so that I can call `sys.exit()` to end the script. Explicitly telling the script to end is a recommended practice, but it is not required because Python interpreter will automatically terminate the script when it reaches the last line. In some script examples online, you may see `quit()` at the end of the script. Both functions send the [SystemExit exception](https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html), which tells the Python interpreter to exit. The `quit()` command comes from the [site](https://docs.python.org/3/library/site.html) package, which is imported during startup in most interactive python environments, like iPython. However, not all computer systems will import site automatically, whereas all python installations come with the **sys** package. The Sys package interfaces your python interpreter with functions on your system. You can avoid errors by using `sys` and end scripts with `sys.exit()` and not `quit()` .

### B.3.3 Creating Git Commit Templates

I found it helpful to set up a template to help remember some of the standards for writing a useful commit message in git. This template pops up every time I enter my commit message. To create a template, create a file called `.gitmessage` and open it with your text editor. Inside the file, for example, you can type:

```
 # Subject: "Applying this commit will ..." (<50 characters)
# What the change to the code is and why the change is made
```

Like in Python, the hashtags ( `#` ) are comments and the trailing text will not be saved in the final version of the commit message. However, they are useful for reminding you of the guidelines.

After creating this file, you need to use `git config` to point to it to our template file. If I saved it in my home directory (e.g. `/home/rebekah` ), the command becomes:

```
 git config --global commit.template /home/rebekah/.gitmessage
```

Now, each time you type `git commit [filename]` , you will see the above template instead of a black text document.

# Appendix C
ADDITIONAL LEARNING RESOURCES

Software Carpentry ([https://software‐carpentry.org/about/](https://software-carpentry.org/about/)) and Data Carpentry (https://datacarpentry.org/about/) offer excellent online tutorials on Python.

The following two books can be helpful as a supplement to this one. They both provide a more in‐depth explanation of Python programming.

*   DeCaria, A. J. and Petty, G.W. (2021). Python programming and visualization for scientists, 2nd ed. Madison, Wisconsin: Sundog Publishing.
*   Lin, J. W.‐B. (2012). A hands‐on introduction to using Python in the atmospheric and oceanic sciences.

In terms of conferences, the *Symposium on Advances in Modeling and Analysis Using Python* at the American Meteorological Society is an excellent way of learning about advances in Python related to the atmospheric sciences. While not specific to Earth sciences, the SciPy conference (e.g., [https://www.scipy2020.scipy.org/](https://www.scipy2020.scipy.org/)) is an opportunity to learn and also share your applications to a more general audience.

There are several online communities that you can join. You may not see yourself as a Python developer at this point, but contributing to either of these communities can help you rapidly increase your skills while benefiting the broader scientific community:

*   **Pangeo project** ([http://pangeo.io/](http://pangeo.io/)). Pangeo is a community that supports an ecosystem of packages that acts as an intersection between science, software, and computer infrastructure. It promotes open, reproducible, and scalable science. The development community provides documentation, develops and maintains software, and deploys computing infrastructure to make scientific research and programming easier. Many of the tools I used in this book are members of its community: **xarray**, **iris**, **dask**, and **Jupyter**. Additionally, cloud computing resources are accessible through their home page.
*   **PyTroll** is a community of developers who curate a collection of packages that prioritizes ease of use and modularization for processing Earth satellite data. In particular, the **pyresample** package (Section 8.3.2) are maintained this community. They also have a series of online tutorials with examples on using their package: https://satpy.readthedocs.io/en/latest/examples/index.html

NASA/GES DISC has released a set of Jupyter notebooks that provide short and clear instruction on ordering, displaying, and analyzing satellite data. These notebooks can be found on [https://disc.gsfc.nasa.gov/](https://disc.gsfc.nasa.gov/) by searching for Jupyter Notebook in “How‐To’s.” Some useful notebooks include:

*   How to download a spatial and variable subset of Level 1B data using OPeNDAP
*   How to read and plot NetCDF MERRA‐2 data in Python
*   How to Read IMERG Data Using Python
*   How to calculate and plot wind speed using MERRA‐2 wind component data using Python
*   How to Use the Web Services API for Subsetting
*   How to Use the Web Services API for Subsetting MERRA‐2 Data
*   How to Use the Web Services API for Dataset Searching
*   How to calculate greenhouse gas growth rates

# Appendix D
TOOLS

## D.1 Text Editors and IDEs

A few suggested IDEs are listed below:

*   **PyCharm.** An IDE that provides code completion, error highlighting, and integration with iPython Notebook. This is a great Python‐centric IDE.
*   **Sublime Text.** A free, lightweight, and multiplatform text editor. Sublime has many add‐on packages as well and one of the most popular ones online.
*   **Atom.** Multiplatform and has a large number of add‐on packages to enhance your code environment. Atom is owned by Github, so it has good integration with version control. Caveat: it uses a lot of memory.
*   **Notepad++.** Windows only. It is simpler to use than Atom and does not use as much memory, as it uses less CPU power (which is only a concern on much older computers).
*   **XCode.** MacOS only. This is a fully loaded IDE, so it contains both a text editor and a command line to edit and run code. This is a good choice if you want to create applications in addition to performing scientific analysis.
*   **Visual Studio Code.** Multiplatform with extension support and an integrated terminal. It has a built‐in command line so you can run the code within the editor and delivers overall good performance. Like Atom, it uses a lot of memory and has a slow launch time.

## D.2 Terminals

To run shell scripts, you will need to open a terminal. A few terminal options are listed below:

*   **CMD Prompt.** The base terminal for Windows.
*   **PuTTy.** The most popular terminal emulator for Windows. Setup can be difficult for beginners, but it is very useful for connecting to remote servers.
*   **Terminal.app.** This is native to the MacOS operating system. While initially Terminal was basic in older versions of MacOS, it has incorporated many of the features of popular third‐party applications. A benefit is that it is already installed on MacOS and usually uses fewer resources than third party applications.
*   **iTerm2.** A third‐party terminal emulator for MacOS, with features such as autocomplete and multiple command lines, either by splitting the window or by opening tabs.

# Appendix E
FINDING, ACCESSING, AND DOWNLOADING SATELLITE DATASETS

To obtain satellite data, it is common for most agencies to have a data‐ordering process. Similar to an online merchant, you shop for your data by searching and adding datasets to your cart. There is no single repository for all data and must require you to register. However, as these services grow more sophisticated, you can subset your data and only download files you need rather than large (and unmanageable) quantities of data.

For near real‐time (NRT) data, there are also FTP services to get access sooner and do not require the manual selection of data. These services include the Precipitation Processing System (PPS) [https://pps.gsfc.nasa.gov/](https://pps.gsfc.nasa.gov/). However, not all data are rapidly available, and all have some form of data **latency**, which is the time between when the observation is made to when data are received by the user.

Below I will show the manual, checkout process for EarthData and CLASS, which are NASA and NOAA’s primary data portals at the time this book was written. Like all web resources, they may evolve over time. All the HDF and netCDF data used in this book are real and accessible through the resources below. As an exercise, pick a file and try to find and order it online.

## E.1 Ordering Data from NASA EarthData

*   Go to [https://earthdata.nasa.gov/](https://earthdata.nasa.gov/)
*   Either browse for data or search. I will search for IMERG data as an example. Type “IMERG” into the search box and click on the search icon (![image](images/bapp05g01.jpg)) to the right.

![Snapshot of typing IMERG into the search box and click on the search icon.](images/AppEuf001.jpg)

*   Select GPM IMERG Final V00x, where x is the highest number available. The V stands for the version number, so selecting the highest number will be the newest version.

![Snapshot of selecting GPM IMERG Final V00x, where x is the highest number available.](images/AppEuf002.jpg)

*   You can subset by time and location using the tools in the top of the menu. For example, below I have made a bound around the Gulf of Mexico and selected dates from August 25–28, 2017, when Hurricane Harvey made landfall in Texas.![Snapshot of making a bound around the Gulf of Mexico and selected dates from August 25–28, 2017, when Hurricane Harvey made land-fall in Texas.](images/AppEuf003.jpg)
*   When you are done, click the `Download All` button.
*   You will be prompted to enter your username and password. If you have not yet registered, you can do this now.![Snapshot of the login page of Earthdata.](images/AppEuf004.jpg)
*   You will be shown all available download options. Click done if you are ready.![Snapshot shows all available download options.](images/AppEuf005.jpg)
*   The order has been placed, and you have been given an ID number. Data are not always immediately available for download, which can be between a few minutes to hours, depending on the size of the order.

## E.2 Ordering Data from NOAA/CLASS

*   Go to [https://www.class.noaa.gov](http://www.class.noaa.gov/)
*   Click on the drop‐down menu (many options will show up).

    ![Snapshot of clicking on the drop-down menu.](images/AppEuf006.jpg)
*   Select JPSS Sounder Products ( `JPSS_SND` ) from the dropdown menu.![Snapshot of selecting JPSS Sounder Products (JPSS_SND) from the dropdown menu.](images/AppEuf007.jpg)
*   Click `Go`![Snapshot of clicking Go.](images/AppEuf008.jpg)
*   Enter your search parameters, e.g., dates, locations. As an example, I selected the latitude and longitude bounds to be 15–35°N and 100–75°W. Under `Datatype` , I selected NUCAPS EDR. For the `Satellite` field, I selected Suomi NPP. Note that NUCAPS data are available from Suomi NPP from 2012 onward and from NOAA‐20 beginning in March 7, 2019.![Snapshot of entering search parameters.](images/AppEuf009.jpg)
*   Below you have two ordering options. `Search` allows you to review before ordering. `Quick Search & Order` skips to the last step without prompting you to review the search results.![Snapshot of the two ordering options.](images/AppEuf010.jpg)
*   If you use `Search` , you will see a list of results. Click on the `Select Datasets` dropdown menu, click `1‐13` , then click `Goto Cart` . Note: You can only order up to 500 datasets per order and will need to make a separate order if there are more or use the search option.![Snapshot of clicking on the Select Datasets dropdown menu, click 1-13, then click Goto Cart.](images/AppEuf011.jpg)
*   If it is your first time ordering from CLASS, you will need to click `Register` and enter your name and email and create a password. If already registered, click `Login` at the top.![Snapshot of clicking Register and enter your name and email and create a password.](images/AppEuf012.jpg)
*   Once you are logged in, the `Register` button will say `Place Order` . Click on place order.![Snapshot of clicking on place order.](images/AppEuf013.jpg)
*   After placing your order, you will receive an order confirmation email. Once the data have been processed, you will receive a second email notifying you that the data are available for download. This email will also include details on how to download the data via ftp or direct link.
